[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F41.9, F43.23, Z63.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally scared me enough to actually make the appointment is, um, I keep having these thoughts that it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. And I know that sounds awful.\n\nIt’s been happening especially after work, like driving home from the clinic — I’m a vet tech, so it’s not like the days are exactly light and breezy anyway, there’s always some emergency or someone crying over their old dog, and then I’m just in the car and I catch myself thinking, what if I just… didn’t turn the wheel? Not that I want to say that out loud, but that’s what got me here.\n\nDoctor: Right now, do you want to die?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say I want to die, exactly. It’s more like I keep thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nAnd then sometimes, driving home from the clinic, I’ll have that thought about the wheel — like, what would happen if I just didn’t turn it. Which sounds so dramatic when I say it, and I hate that, because I’m usually the one at work keeping it together while everyone else is falling apart over a sick cat or a client yelling about a bill. But yeah… those thoughts are there, and they scared me.\n\nDoctor: When you have the thought about not turning the wheel, do you want to crash the car?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I want to crash, no… I mean, not in a clear, “I’m going to do this” kind of way. It’s more like the thought flashes in my head when I’m driving home from the clinic, like, what if I just didn’t turn the wheel? And then I scare myself because that’s not normal, right?\n\nMostly it’s that feeling of… it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Then I start thinking like that in the car, and I hate even saying it because I’m supposed to be careful, I’m the one telling people how to keep their animals safe, and meanwhile I’m having these awful thoughts on the way home.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after something specific happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it kind of got really bad around when we took in our relative. That’s been about a month now, I think.\n\nIt’s hard because it’s not just one thing, it’s like the whole house changed, and suddenly everything feels… loud, even when nobody’s talking. And I keep thinking I should be handling it better, because people handle way worse, you know? At the clinic we see families falling apart over their pets all the time and I’m usually the steady one, but at home it’s just been this whole intense up-and-down crisis since they came to stay with us. About a month.\n\nDoctor: If your relative moved out and the situation at home settled down, do you think you’d still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, that if they moved out everything would just… go back to normal. It would help, I’m sure it would, because this past month has made everything feel unbearable.\n\nBut honestly, it was already there before that. This heaviness has been hanging over me for… I don’t know, around 11 months now. I kept blaming work, or getting older, or just being tired — at the clinic there’s always some emergency or someone crying in an exam room, and you sort of learn to shove your own stuff down. But it didn’t start with them moving in. That just made it feel like I couldn’t get any air anymore.\n\nDoctor: Before this past 11 months, was there ever another time in your life when you went through a similar period of feeling this depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve had hard times before, like everybody has, and there were periods where I wasn’t myself, but whether it was like this… I’m not sure.\n\nIt’s funny, I can remember random things so clearly, like one old dog we had at the clinic years ago who’d only let me trim his nails if I sang to him, but then when you ask me to line up my own moods over my life, it all gets blurry. I guess I’d have to think about it more. Right now this past year is just taking up so much space in my head that it’s hard to compare.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when things at home are relatively calm, do you still feel that same heaviness?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Even when it’s calm, it’s still there. It’s like… I don’t know, like the color got washed out of everything.\n\nThe house can be perfectly normal, people just making dinner or watching TV, and I’m standing there feeling like there’s this weight in me. Sometimes I’ll go into the laundry room and just cry because I can’t let anyone see it. I don’t want to make everything harder, but I feel so hopeless, and it doesn’t really lift just because things are quiet for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. That’s actually one of the things that scares me.\n\nI haven’t touched my camera in ages, and that used to be the thing I’d reach for without even thinking. I’d see the light hit something in the yard or one of the clinic cats making some ridiculous face and I’d want to take a picture. Now I just… don’t care enough to pick it up.\n\nAnd the bird feeders — I still notice them, sort of, but I don’t check them with any real interest. Same with the garden. Usually I’d be thinking ahead, what’s coming up next, what needs moving, what might bloom, and now it’s like someone else’s yard. Those things used to make me feel like myself, and now they’re just sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know I’ve had stretches where I was down or kind of numb, but whether it was nearly every day for two weeks… I honestly don’t know.\n\nIt’s strange, because I can remember things from work years ago in ridiculous detail, like a parrot that used to mimic the phone ring and make everyone jump, but with my own mood it’s harder to pin down. I didn’t keep track of it. I’d probably just tell myself I was tired or stressed and keep going.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t say clearly, no.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep has been awful this year.\n\nI either lie there wide awake, just replaying everything I did wrong — things I said, things I forgot, even stupid little moments from work that probably nobody else remembers. It’s like my brain decides bedtime is when we’re going to review the entire list.\n\nOr I wake up before dawn already exhausted. Sometimes I stay in bed longer because I think maybe if I just rest more it’ll help, but it doesn’t. I get up feeling like I haven’t really slept, and then I can’t run or function the way I normally would. It’s not just “I’m a little tired,” it’s like I’m starting the day already drained.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I hate saying that, but yes.\n\nI keep telling myself I’m a terrible person, especially for feeling resentful about how disrupted everything at home has been. I know families are messy and people need things, and I’m supposed to be patient — I mean, at work I can handle a terrified dog trying to climb the walls or a client yelling because they’re scared, and somehow I can stay calm there. But at home, if I have one impatient look or some sharp little comment, I replay it for hours.\n\nAnd then it turns into this whole… proof, in my head, that I’m the problem. Like my husband and the family would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me being like this. I know that sounds awful, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean… I get through work, mostly, because there are routines and checklists and animals right in front of me, and you can’t exactly drift off when you’re holding a squirming cat. Although cats are funny that way — some of them act like they’re made of springs, and then five minutes later they’re completely limp like they’ve given up on the world. Sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nConcentrating… I haven’t really been tracking it. Sometimes I feel scattered, but I don’t know if that’s concentration exactly or just having too much in my head. I can still focus when I absolutely have to, I think. So I’m not sure I’d say it’s been a clear problem, but I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure, honestly.\n\nBefore this year, I know I’ve had rough patches, like everyone does, but whether it was nearly every day for two weeks — I don’t know how to measure that looking back. Time gets kind of blurry for me that way. I can remember specific things, like appointments or animals I worked with, but my own mood over a stretch of time is harder to pin down.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say yes if I’m just guessing. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed, I think. It’s kind of all over the place, but lately I mostly just pick at things — like toast, or I’ll have coffee and tell myself that counts as breakfast, which it obviously doesn’t.\n\nThen I’ll get to the end of a clinic shift and realize I basically haven’t eaten all day. It’s weird because at work I’m still doing things, feeding the animals, making sure they’ve got what they need — and some of them are on these very specific diets, like you have to measure everything — and meanwhile I’m just… not doing that for myself. So yes, my appetite hasn’t been normal.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time when you felt like this for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Before this past year… I can remember periods where my eating was off, but I can’t say clearly whether it was like this for two full weeks in a row.\n\nIt’s funny, I can remember exactly what some dogs were eating years ago — prescription food, allergies, how picky they were — but with myself it’s much fuzzier. I don’t keep track of my meals that way. So I don’t want to give you a definite yes or no. I’m just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in most things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I know you asked before this past year, but what I can say clearly is how it’s been now. Most days it feels like the color has drained out of everything, even if the house is noisy or busy around me.\n\nI’ll be doing laundry or something ordinary, and then I end up crying in the laundry room because I don’t want anyone to see how hopeless I feel. It’s strange, because there can be people around and things happening, but inside it’s just… flat and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time when you felt depressed or lost interest in most things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m not answering that very cleanly, am I? Before this past year… I’m having trouble pinning it down.\n\nWhat I can say is that this feeling now is very clear to me — most days it’s like the color has drained out of everything, even if the house is busy and there’s noise and people around. I’ll go into the laundry room and end up crying in there because I can’t stand anyone seeing how hopeless I feel.\n\nI know you’re asking if that happened before too, for two weeks or more, and I’m sorry… I’m not sure I can say that for sure.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in most things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to get a straight yes or no from me. Before this past year, I just can’t say for sure.\n\nI’ve had hard stretches in my life, but whether it was nearly every day for two weeks, and whether it was really depression or just being exhausted or distracted… I don’t know. I remember work details better than my own moods, honestly. Like I can tell you about an old patient’s feeding schedule from years ago, but not exactly how many days in a row I felt low.\n\nSo… I don’t want to make something up. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, do you remember ever having a period when you felt depressed or lost interest in most things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to remember, but I honestly don’t know. Before this past year, everything feels kind of mixed together in my head.\n\nI can remember things from work so clearly sometimes — like which animal needed what medication, or how a certain doctor liked things set up — but when it comes to my own moods, I get fuzzy. I know there were times I wasn’t doing great, but whether it was nearly every day for two weeks or more… I don’t want to guess and give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s… it’s definitely affecting daily life.\n\nAt the clinic I’ve started making mistakes, which is not like me. I’ll miss something small or have to double-check things over and over, and then I get so embarrassed because I used to be the person everyone relied on. It’s like my brain decides bedtime is when we’re going to review the entire list.\n\nOr I wake up before dawn already exhausted. Sometimes I stay in bed longer because I think maybe if I just rest more it’ll help, but it doesn’t. I get up feeling like I haven’t really slept, and then I can’t run or function the way I normally would. It’s not just “I’m a little tired,” it’s like I’m starting the day already drained.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I hate saying that, but yes.\n\nI keep telling myself I’m a terrible person, especially for feeling resentful about how disrupted everything at home has been. I know families are messy and people need things, and I’m supposed to be patient — I mean, at work I can handle a terrified dog trying to climb the walls or a client yelling because they’re scared, and somehow I can stay calm there. But at home, if I have one impatient look or some sharp little comment, I replay it for hours.\n\nAnd then it turns into this whole… proof, in my head, that I’m the problem. Like my husband and the family would be better off if they didn’t have to deal with me being like this. I know that sounds awful, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean… I get through work, mostly, because there are routines and checklists and animals right in front of me, and you can’t exactly drift off when you’re holding a squirming cat. Although cats are funny that way — some of them act like they’re made of springs, and then five minutes later they’re completely limp like they’ve given up on the world. Sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nConcentrating… I haven’t really been tracking it. Sometimes I feel scattered, but I don’t know if that’s concentration exactly or just having too much in my head. I can still focus when I absolutely have to, I think. So I’m not sure I’d say it’s been a clear problem, but I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure, honestly.\n\nBefore this year, I know I’ve had rough patches, like everyone does, but whether it was nearly every day for two weeks — I don’t know how to measure that looking back. Time gets kind of blurry for me that way. I can remember specific things, like appointments or animals I worked with, but my own mood over a stretch of time is harder to pin down.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say yes if I’m just guessing. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed, I think. It’s kind of all over the place, but lately I mostly just pick at things — like toast, or I’ll have coffee and tell myself that counts as breakfast, which it obviously doesn’t.\n\nThen I’ll get to the end of a clinic shift and realize I basically haven’t eaten all day. It’s weird because at work I’m still doing things, feeding the animals, making sure they’ve got what they need — and some of them are on these very specific diets, like you have to measure everything — and meanwhile I’m just… not doing that for myself. So yes, my appetite hasn’t been normal.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time when you felt like this for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Before this past year… I can remember periods where my eating was off, but I can’t say clearly whether it was like this for two full weeks in a row.\n\nIt’s funny, I can remember exactly what some dogs were eating years ago — prescription food, allergies, how picky they were — but with myself it’s much fuzzier. I don’t keep track of my meals that way. So I don’t want to give you a definite yes or no. I’m just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in most things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I know you asked before this past year, but what I can say clearly is how it’s been now. Most days it feels like the color has drained out of everything, even if the house is noisy or busy around me.\n\nI’ll be doing laundry or something ordinary, and then I end up crying in the laundry room because I don’t want anyone to see how hopeless I feel. It’s strange, because there can be people around and things happening, but inside it’s just… flat and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time when you felt depressed or lost interest in most things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m not answering that very cleanly, am I? Before this past year… I’m having trouble pinning it down.\n\nWhat I can say is that this feeling now is very clear to me — most days it’s like the color has drained out of everything, even if the house is busy and there’s noise and people around. I’ll go into the laundry room and end up crying in there because I can’t stand anyone seeing how hopeless I feel.\n\nI know you’re asking if that happened before too, for two weeks or more, and I’m sorry… I’m not sure I can say that for sure.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in most things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to get a straight yes or no from me. Before this past year, I just can’t say for sure.\n\nI’ve had hard stretches in my life, but whether it was nearly every day for two weeks, and whether it was really depression or just being exhausted or distracted… I don’t know. I remember work details better than my own moods, honestly. Like I can tell you about an old patient’s feeding schedule from years ago, but not exactly how many days in a row I felt low.\n\nSo… I don’t want to make something up. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, do you remember ever having a period when you felt depressed or lost interest in most things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to remember, but I honestly don’t know. Before this past year, everything feels kind of mixed together in my head.\n\nI can remember things from work so clearly sometimes — like which animal needed what medication, or how a certain doctor liked things set up — but when it comes to my own moods, I get fuzzy. I know there were times I wasn’t doing great, but whether it was nearly every day for two weeks or more… I don’t want to guess and give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s… it’s definitely affecting daily life.\n\nAt the clinic I’ve started making mistakes, which is not like me. I’ll miss something small or have to double-check things over and over, and then I get so embarrassed because I used to be the person everyone relied on. And then I start thinking about how one mistake can matter so much with an animal, you know, even something little with a dose or a chart, and I just spiral.\n\nI’ve also been avoiding friends. I don’t trust myself to be normal. I’m afraid I’ll snap at someone over nothing, or I’ll just start crying and not be able to stop.\n\nAt home, my husband has had to take over a lot — bills, meals, just keeping things going. And sometimes when my thoughts get really dark, he’s the one making sure I’m safe. So yes, it’s been hard to function."
